Genericity Through Stratification DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX Conference: Thirty-Ninth Annual ACM/IEEE Symposium on Logic in Computer Science; 8–12 July 2024; Tallinn, Estonia Price: 15.00 ISBN: 978-1-4503-XXXX-X/18/06 CCS: Mathematics of computing Lambda calculus
Victor Arrial, Giulio Guerrieri, Delia Kesner

Abstract
. A fundamental issue in the λ \lambda -calculus is to find appropriate notions for meaningfulness . It is well-known that in the call-by-name λ \lambda -calculus (CbN) the meaningful terms can be identified with the solvable ones, and that this notion is not appropriate in the call-by-value λ \lambda -calculus (CbV). This paper validates the challenging claim that yet another notion, previously introduced in the literature as potential valuability , appropriately represents meaningfulness in CbV. Akin to CbN, this claim is corroborated by proving two essential properties. The first one is genericity , stating that meaningless subterms have no bearing on evaluating normalizing terms. To prove this (which was an open problem), we use a novel approach based on stratified reduction, indifferently applicable to CbN and CbV, and in a quantitative way. The second property concerns consistency of the smallest congruence relation resulting from equating all meaningless terms. While the consistency result is not new, we provide the first direct operational proof of it. We also show that such a congruence has a unique consistent and maximal extension, which coincides with a well-known notion of observational equivalence. Our results thus supply the formal concepts and tools that validate the informal notion of meaningfulness underlying CbN and CbV.



1.
Introduction

Meaningfulness and Meaninglessness

One of the aims of programming language theory is to establish
semantics describing the meaning of computer programs (see

(
Hutton 2023
)
for a survey). It is then natural to search for
criteria that distinguish, among syntactically correct (
i.e.
compiling) programs, the meaningful ones (those computing something)
from the meaningless ones (those not computing anything, for instance
because they always diverge for every input).

In the context of the pure untyped
\lambda
-calculus (the
prototypical kernel common to all functional programming languages and
many proof assistants), this question has been extensively studied
since the 70s

(
Scott 1970
;
Scott and Strachey 1971
;
Barendregt 1971
;
Wadsworth 1971
;
Scott 1972
;
Hyland 1975
;
Wadsworth 1976
;
Barendregt92a
)
.
Setting up a semantic for the
\lambda
-calculus amounts to fixing an

equivalence
relation
\equiv
on
\lambda
-terms, with the
underlying intuition that two equivalent
\lambda
-terms share the

same meaning
. Two primary requirements for
\equiv

must be considered:

(1)

Invariance under computation:

\equiv
must include
\beta
-equivalence
=_{\beta}
, which is the
reflexive, symmetric and transitive closure of
\beta
-reduction. In
other words, if
t=_{\beta}u
then
t\equiv u
. Roughly, if
t=_{\beta}u
then
t
and
u
have a common
\beta
-reduct (by the
Church-Rosser theorem), meaning that
t
and
u
represent different
stages of the same computation and thus share the same meaning.

(2)

Compositionality:

\equiv

must be preserved when plugging
\lambda
-terms into the same
context,
i.e.
if
t\equiv u
then
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\equiv\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>

for every context
\mathtt{C}
. Essentially this means that the
meaning of a compound
\lambda
-term is defined solely by the
meaning of its subterms.

Equivalence relations fulfilling

Items

1

and

2
are usually called

\lambda
-theories

(
Barendregt and Manzonetto 2022
)
. In programming language theory, there are at least three more
desiderata for them:

(3)

Consistency:
Equivalence
\equiv

must not equate all
\lambda
-terms,
i.e.

t\not\equiv u
for at least two
\lambda
-terms
t
and
u
,
otherwise the semantics would be trivial and convey no information.

(4)

Sensibility:
All meaningless

\lambda
-terms should be equated by
\equiv
. Essentially, there
should be no semantic difference between two
\lambda
-terms that
have no meaning!

(5)

Genericity:
Meaningless subterms are
computationally irrelevant —in the sense that they do not play any role— in the
evaluation of
\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-terms

Genericity says that a meaningless subterm
t
of a
\beta
-normalizing term

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
does not
impact on the computation from
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
to its
\beta
-normal form
u
, and
hence
t
is
generic
, that is, it can be replaced by any other

\lambda
-term
s
and still
\mathtt{C}\!\left<s\right>

\beta
-reduces to
u
.

A
\lambda
-theory gives insights into denotational and
operational semantics of the
\lambda
-calculus,
e.g.
it may stem from
a denotational model (by equating exactly the
\lambda
-terms with the
same interpretation), or may reflect a particular observational
equivalence (by equating exactly the observationally equivalent

\lambda
-terms). Two
\lambda
-terms
t
and
u
are

observationally equivalent
with respect to a notion of
observable if
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
reduces to an observable exactly when

\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
does. In programming language theory, this is a way to
determine when programs can be considered equivalent as they have the
same behavior.

A Naive Notion of Meaningfulness

A naive approach to set a semantics for the pure untyped

\lambda
-calculus is to define the meaning of a

\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-term as its normal form, and equating
all
\lambda
-terms that do not
\beta
-normalize. The underlying idea
is that, as
\beta
-reduction represents evaluation and a normal form
stands for its outcome, all
non
-
\beta
-normalizing

\lambda
-terms are considered as meaningless (intuitively, they
represent non-terminating programs).

Evidence that this naive approach to semantics is flawed is that it
equates every fixed point combinator (any
\lambda
-term
Y
such that

t(Yt)=_{\beta}t
for every
\lambda
-term
t
) with the prototypical
diverging
\lambda
-term
\Omega=(\lambda x.xx)\lambda x.xx
. While both

Y
and
\Omega
fail to
\beta
-normalize, the former is essential
for representing recursive functions in the
\lambda
-calculus,
suggesting it should carry some non-trivial semantics, while
\Omega

is indisputably meaningless. This suggests the idea that the

\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-terms are not the only meaningful

\lambda
-terms.

Indeed, as thoroughly discussed in
(
Barendregt 1984
, Chapter 2)
,
the naive approach has some major drawbacks. In particular, any

\lambda
-theory equating all non-
\beta
-normalizing
\lambda
-terms
is
inconsistent
: it equates all
\lambda
-terms, not just the
meaningless ones!

Solvable as meaningful

In the 70s, Wadsworth
(
Wadsworth 1971
;
Wadsworth 1976
)
and
Barendregt

(
Barendregt 1971
;
Barendregt 1973
;
Barendregt 1977
;
Barendregt 1984
)
showed
that the meaningful
\lambda
-terms can be identified with the

solvable
ones. The definition of solvability seems
technical: a
\lambda
-term
t
is
solvable
if there is a
special kind of context, called
head
context
\mathtt{H}
,
sending
t
to the identity function
\mathtt{I}=\lambda z.z
, meaning that

\mathtt{H}\!\left<t\right>

\beta
-reduces to
\mathtt{I}
. Roughly, a solvable

\lambda
-term
t
may be divergent, but its diverging subterms can be
removed via interaction with a suitable head context
\mathtt{H}
which
cannot merely discard
t
. For instance,
x\Omega
is divergent but
solvable (take the head context
\mathtt{H}=(\lambda x.\diamond)\lambda y.\mathtt{I}
). It turns out that
unsolvable

\lambda
-terms constitutes a strict subset of the
non-
\beta
-normalizing ones (notably all fixed point combinators are
solvable). Moreover, the smallest
\lambda
-theory that equates all
unsolvable
\lambda
-terms —let us call it
\mathcal{H}
— is

consistent
(
Item

3
). Following the key idea in Barendregt’s book
(
Barendregt 1984
)
, if we identify

solvable as meaningful
, and
hence
unsolvable as
meaningless
, we have that
:

•

The
\lambda
-theory
\mathcal{H}
gives rise to a

sensible
semantics (
Item

4
), and

•

Genericity
holds (
Item

5
), meaning that if

t
is
unsolvable
and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>

\beta
-reduces to

some

\beta
-normal term
u
for some context
\mathtt{C}
,
then
\mathtt{C}\!\left<s\right>

\beta
-reduces to
u
for
every

\lambda
-term
s
.

Not only is genericity an interesting operational property
per
se
, but it is also a crucial ingredient to prove consistency of

\mathcal{H}
in a
direct way
(not relying on consistency of other
\lambda
-theories), following
(
Barendregt 1984
)
.

The fact that
Items

3
,
4
and
5
hold is
the main rationale for embracing solvability as a formal
representation of the informal notion of meaningfulness in the pure
untyped
\lambda
-calculus, as extensively discussed
in
(
Barendregt 1984
)
. This
semantic approach gives rise to an elegant and successful theory of
the
\lambda
-calculus, see

(
Wadsworth 1971
;
Barendregt 1971
;
Barendregt 1973
;
Barendregt 1977
;
Wadsworth 1976
;
Coppo and Dezani-Ciancaglini 1978
;
Coppo and Dezani-Ciancaglini 1980
;
Barendregt 1984
;
Barendregt92a
;
Barendregt and Manzonetto 2022
)
.

Call-by-Name & Call-by-Value

Solvability has been extensively studied in the standard pure untyped

\lambda
-calculus, which we refer to as
call-by-name
(CbN for
short). This approach does not require the argument to be evaluated
before being passed to the calling function. In the 70s,
Plotkin
(
Plotkin 1975
)
introduced the
call-by-value
(CbV, for
short) variant of the pure untyped
\lambda
-calculus, which is closer
to actual implementation of functional programming languages and proof
assistants. The key distinction lies in its reduction rule
\beta_{v}
:
a restriction of
\beta
-reduction that allows a redex to be fired
only when the argument is a
value
, that is, a variable or an
abstraction. Among
\lambda
-theories, we distinguish

\lambda_{n}
-theories
(for CbN) and
\lambda_{v}
-theories

(for CbV) if their invariance is under
\beta
- and

\beta_{v}
-reduction, respectively.

It is tempting to adapt the notion of solvability to CbV, by simply
replacing
\beta
-reduction with
\beta_{v}
-reduction in its
definition. It has been proved that CbV solvability has appealing
properties
(
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
Guerrieri et al. 2017
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
.
Unfortunately, as it is well-known,
any
\lambda_{v}
-theory (
Items

1

and

2
) that
equates all CbV unsolvable
\lambda
-terms (
Item

4
)
cannot be consistent (
Item

3
), see

e.g.

(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
. Furthermore, Accattoli and
Guerrieri
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
have showed that genericity
(
Item

5
) fails for CbV unsolvable
\lambda
-terms.
Therefore, meaningfulness in CbV cannot be identified with CbV
solvability, unless we are willing to relinquish to the desired
properties outlined in

Items

3
,
4
and
5
. Identifying
appropriate notions to capture CbV meaningful
\lambda
-terms and
formally validating these notions is a longstanding and
challenging open question.

Our First Contribution: Scrutable as Meaningful in CbV

Facing these negative results, our paper investigates the notion of
meaningfulness in CbV using a different approach, in the vein of what
Barendregt
(
Barendregt 1984
)
did for CbN. Indeed, we look for a
suitable of CbV meaningfulness such that
the smallest
\lambda_{v}
-theory it
generates
(according to

Items

4
,
1
and
2
) fulfills the
other
desired properties (
Items

3

and

5
)
.

Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca
(
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
)
introduced
the notion of
potentially valuability
for CbV, also studied in

(
Paolini et al. 2006
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
García-Pérez and Nogueira 2016
)

and renamed
(CbV) scrutability
in

(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
: a
\lambda
-term is
scrutable

if there is a
head
context
\mathtt{H}
sending
t
to some
value, that is, such that
\mathtt{H}\!\left<t\right>

\beta_{v}
-reduces
to
some value.
Scrutable
\lambda
-terms form a
proper superset
of the CbV
solvable ones, for instance
\lambda x.\Omega
is scrutable but not CbV
solvable. Akin to CbV solvability, scrutability has
appealing characterizations

(
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
;
Paolini et al. 2006
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
Guerrieri et al. 2017
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
.
But there is more than that.
Indeed, one of the main contributions of our paper is to prove that we
can identify
scrutable as CbV meaningful
, and hence

inscrutable as CbV meaningless
, in that we prove the following
properties:

•

Genericity
(
Item

5
): in CbV, if
t
is
CbV
meaningless
and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
reduces to a
normal form
u
for some context
\mathtt{C}
, then

\mathtt{C}\!\left<s\right>
reduces to
u
for

every
\lambda
-term
s
;

•

Consistency
(
Item

3
): the smallest

\lambda_{v}
-theory (
Items

1

and

2
) that
equates all CbV meaningless
\lambda
-terms (
Item

4
)
is consistent.

The main and non-trivial challenge is to prove genericity, conjectured but not proved
in
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
. Concerning consistency, it is
already proved in the literature —or can be easily derived— from the
consistency of some related

\lambda_{v}
-theories
(
Egidi et al. 1992
;
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
;
Accattoli and Lancelot 2024
)
.
But we provide the first direct operational proof of consistency,
using genericity.

Our Second Contribution: Stratified Genericity, Modularly

We claim that
our approach to prove genericity is
finer
than the ones in the
literature, and above all
robust
, in that it indifferently
applies to CbN and CbV in a modular way.

Why our approach is finer? What is called genericity in the
literature, is called here
full genericity
, because it is about
the behavior of meaningless
\lambda
-terms that are plugged in

full
contexts (
i.e.
contexts without any restriction) when they
reduce
to
full
normal forms (
i.e.
normal forms for unrestricted
reduction). We also study
non-full
genericity, by considering
non-full contexts and non-full normal forms, which amounts to looking at
contexts and normal forms until a certain
depth
. Thus, a
natural notion of
stratified genericity
arises, depending on
how deep we consider contexts and normal forms. Our approach proves
stratified genericity for
any
depth, the full case (the usual
one considered in the literature) being only a special case where
there is no limit to the depth. Another special case is
surface
genericity
, that is, stratified genericity where the depth is
0
.

Moreover, our approach does not only bring forward
qualitative

aspects of genericity, but also
quantitative
ones: if
t
is
meaningless and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
normalizes to
u
for some context

\mathtt{C}
in
m
steps, then
\mathtt{C}\!\left<s\right>
normalizes to
u
for

every

\lambda
-term
s
in exactly
m
steps. Thus,
t
is not
only qualitative irrelevant, but also quantitatively irrelevant.

Our third contribution:
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}^{*}

in CbV

The literature on
\lambda_{n}
-theories (for CbN) is
vast
(
Barendregt 1984
;
Barendregt92a
;
Berline 2000
;
Berline et al. 2007
;
Berline et al. 2009
;
Breuvart 2014
;
Breuvart et al. 2016
;
Barendregt and Manzonetto 2022
)
,
and the set of
\lambda_{n}
-theories forms a very rich
mathematical structure.
In particular, the aforesaid
\mathcal{H}
has a unique maximal consistent extension called
\mathcal{H}^{*}

(
Hyland 1975
)
.

Concerning CbV, instead, the study of
\lambda_{v}
-theories is still in
its early
stages
(
Egidi et al. 1992
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
,
also because of the intricacies of CbV semantics. We already mentioned
that the smallest
\lambda_{v}
-theory equating all CbV meaningless

\lambda
-terms is consistent. As a further contribution, thanks again
to genericity, we prove that such a
\lambda_{v}
-theory has a unique
maximal consistent extension, which coincides with a well known notion
of CbV observational equivalence
(
Plotkin 1975
)
, the one where the
notion of observable is to reduce to some value.

All these results in CbV are perfectly analogous to well known results
for CbN
(
Hyland 1975
;
Barendregt 1984
;
Barendregt and Manzonetto 2022
)
,
suggesting that there is a sort of
canonicity
in the proposal
to identify CbV meaningfulness with scrutability.

Methodological Remark

In this paper, we do not work in the standard CbN
(
CHURCH 1941
)
and
CbV
(
Plotkin 1975
)

\lambda
-calculi, but in their respective
variants with
explicit substitution
s and reduction
at a
distance

(
Accattoli and Kesner 2010
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
)
. Indeed, our technique for both CbN and CbV
relies on an existing
operational characterization
of
meaningfulness: a
\lambda
-term
t
is meaningful if and only if a

certain
reduction starting at
t
terminates. Such a reduction
is given by the notion of
head reduction
for CbN
(
Wadsworth 1971
;
Wadsworth 1976
)
, which is
internal
to the
calculus (a subreduction of
\beta
). For CbV, instead, it is not
possible to have an operational characterization of meaningfulness
internally to Plotkin’s original CbV
\lambda
-calculus, as already
remarked by Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca
(
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
)
. This weakness of Plotkin’s CbV is widely
accepted in the literature on CbV, which provides many alternative
proposals to extend CbV
(
Grégoire and Leroy 2002
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2016
;
Herbelin and Zimmermann 2009
)

in such a way that an internal characterization of scrutability
becomes possible. Among them we chose the formalism in
(
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
)
,
called here
dCBV
, as it has good rewriting properties. For
uniformity, we also took its CbN version
(
Accattoli and Kesner 2012
)
.

Outline

Section

2
presents the
dCBV
-Calculus and
a first result of (qualitative) surface genericity. A general
methodology to prove quantitative full genericity is introduced
in
Section

3
, and we instantiate it to the
dCBV
-Calculus
in
Section

4
. Call-by-name genericity is
developed in
Section

5
, still using the methodology proposed
in
Section

3
.
Section

6
presents the results of
consistency and sensibility for the associated theories.

Section

7
discusses related works and concludes.
Omitted proofs are in the Appendix.

2.
The
dCBV
-Calculus

This section introduces the syntax and operational semantics of
the
dCBV
-calculus, including the new notion of
stratified
reduction
(a key ingredient of our approach). We also recall
the
\mathcal{V}
type system
(
Bucciarelli et al. 2020
)
, another key tool in this work.
Adequacy of the
dCBV
-calculus versus non-adequacy of Plotkin’s CbV
is also discussed, to justify the use of
dCBV
.
At the end of the section, a first qualitative surface genericity result
(
Theorem

2.8
) is obtained
thanks to the type system
.

Syntax

We present the syntax of the
dCBV
Calculus, a call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus with explicit
substitutions originally introduced in
(
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
)
, where it is
called the
Value Substitution Calculus
. Given a countably
infinite set
\mathcal{X}
of variables
x,y,z,\dots
, the sets of
terms (
\Lambda
) and values (
\Upsilon
) are given by the
following inductive definitions:

\begin{array}[]{r rcl}\textbf{(Terms)}&{\tt t},u&\;\Coloneqq&v\;|\;tu\;|\;t[x\backslash u]\\
\textbf{(Values)}&v&\;\Coloneqq&x\in\mathcal{X}\;|\;\lambda x.t\end{array}

The set
\Lambda
includes
variables

x
,

abstractions

\lambda x.t
,
applications

tu

and
closures

t[x\backslash u]
, the latter representing a pending

explicit substitution (ES)

[x\backslash u]
on
t
. Terms without
ES are called
ES-free
. Abstractions
\lambda x.t
and closures

t[x\backslash u]
bind the variable
x
in
t
.

The set of
free
variables
{\tt fv}(t)
of a term
t
is
defined as expected, in particular
{\tt fv}(\lambda x.t)={\tt fv}(t)\backslash\{x\}
and
{\tt fv}(t[x\backslash u])={\tt fv}(u)\cup({\tt fv}(t)\backslash\{x\})
. The usual notion of

\alpha
-conversion
(
Barendregt 1984
)
is extended to the whole set

\Lambda
, and terms are identified up to
\alpha
-conversion.
For example
x[x\backslash\lambda y.y]=z[z\backslash\lambda x.x]
. We denote
by
t\{x\backslash u\}
the term obtained by the usual (capture-avoiding)
meta-level substitution of the term
u
for all free occurrences of
the variable
x
in the term
t
.

Notation 0.

We set
\mathtt{I}\coloneqq\lambda x.x
and
\Delta\coloneqq\lambda x.xx
and
\Omega\coloneqq\Delta\Delta
.

(Stratified) Contexts and Equality

The operational semantics of
dCBV
is often specified by
means of a
weak
reduction relation
(
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
)
that forbids
evaluation under abstractions, called here
surface
reduction.
In this work however, we equip
dCBV
with a much finer

stratified
operational semantics which generalizes surface
reduction to different
levels
. For example, given a term
t=\lambda x.(u_{1}(\lambda y.u_{2}))
, reduction at level
1
allows
to evaluate inside one binder
\lambda
, but not inside two or more,
so that
u_{1}
can be evaluated while
u_{2}
cannot. This extended
semantics sheds a light on the layering intrinsically standing out in
the study of meaningfulness. From now on, we denote by
\mathbb{N}^{\omega}

the set
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
(with
i<\omega
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
), which will be used to
index all the forthcoming notions with respect to a level.

To define such stratified operational semantics, we need a fine-grained notion of context (a terms containing exactly one hole
\diamond
).
The set of
list contexts

(\mathtt{L})
and

stratified contexts

(\mathtt{V}_{k})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
are inductively defined as follows (where
i\in\mathbb{N}
):

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{L}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\\[5.69046pt]
\mathtt{V}_{0}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{0}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{V}_{0}\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{0}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{V}_{0}]\\
\mathtt{V}_{i+1}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{V}_{i}\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{i+1}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{V}_{i+1}\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{i+1}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{V}_{i+1}]\\
\mathtt{V}_{\omega},\mathtt{C}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{V}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{\omega}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{V}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathtt{V}_{\omega}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{V}_{\omega}]\end{array}\end{array}

A stratified context is
ES-free
if it is without ES.
Special cases of stratified contexts are

surface contexts
(whose hole is not in the
scope of any
\lambda
) and
full contexts
(the
restriction-free one-hole contexts, also denoted by
\mathtt{C}
): the former (resp. the latter) are
stratified contexts for the minimal (resp. transfinite) level,
i.e.

\mathtt{V}_{0}
(resp.
\mathtt{V}_{\omega}
). In a stratified context

\mathtt{V}_{k}
, the hole can occur everywhere as long as its
depth is at most
k
, so that in
\mathtt{V}_{\omega}
the
hole can occur everywhere and in
\mathtt{V}_{0}
it cannot occur under
any abstraction. Thus for example
y\,(\lambda x.\diamond)
is
in
\mathtt{V}_{1}
, and thus in every
\mathtt{V}_{k}
with
k\geq 1
, including
\mathtt{V}_{\omega}
, but it is not in

\mathtt{V}_{0}
. For all
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, we write

\mathtt{V}_{k}\!\left<t\right>
for the term obtained by replacing the hole in

\mathtt{V}_{k}
with the term
t
, and similarly for
\mathtt{L}
.

We define the
stratified equality
comparing terms up to a given
depth by the following inductive rules (where
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
i\in\mathbb{N})
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-5.69046pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 8.76215pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\!\scriptsize$({\tt var})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$x\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}x$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 11.38092pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.65398pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\!\scriptsize$({\tt app})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$t_{1}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{1}$}}\hbox{$t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{2}$}}}}\hbox{$t_{1}t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{1}u_{2}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 11.38092pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 22.36917pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\!\scriptsize$({\tt es})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$t_{1}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{1}$}}\hbox{$t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{2}$}}}}\hbox{$t_{1}[x\backslash t_{2}]\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u_{1}[x\backslash u_{2}]$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[11.38092pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 23.24655pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt abs})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\lambda x.t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}\lambda y.u$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 22.76228pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 22.48611pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\!\scriptsize$({\tt abs})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}u$}}\hbox{$\lambda x.t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}{\approx}\lambda x.u$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 22.76228pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 15.99304pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\!\scriptsize$({\tt abs})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u$}}\hbox{$\lambda x.t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}\lambda x.u$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

Notice that
t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u
if and only if
t=u
.

Example 2.1.

Let
t_{0}:=(\lambda x.x(\lambda y.x))z
and
t_{1}:=(\lambda x.x(\lambda z.z))z
. We have
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}t_{1}
and
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 1}{\approx}t_{1}
but not
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}t_{1}
for
any
k\geq 2
.

(Stratified) Operational Semantics.

The notions
of contexts already established, we can now equip
dCBV
with an operational semantics. The following rewriting
rules are the base components of such operational semantics. Any term
having the shape of the left-hand side of one of these three rules is
called a
redex
.

\begin{array}[]{c c c}\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.s\right>t\;\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\;\mathtt{L}\!\left<s[x\backslash t]\right>&&t[x\backslash\mathtt{L}\!\left<v\right>]\;\mapsto_{{\tt sV}}\;\mathtt{L}\!\left<t\{x\backslash v\}\right>\end{array}

Rules
{\tt dB}
and
{\tt sV}
are assumed to be

capture free
, so no free variable of
t
is captured by the
context
\mathtt{L}
. The
{\tt dB}
-rule fires a
\beta
-redex
(with no restriction on the argument
t
) and generates an ES. The

{\tt sV}
-rule encapsulates the call-by-value behavior: it fires
an ES provided that its argument is a
value

v
, possibly
wrapped in a list context
\mathtt{L}
. In both rewrite rules,
reduction acts
at a distance

(
Accattoli and Kesner 2010
)
: the main constructors
involved in the rule can be separated by a finite—possibly
empty—list
\mathtt{L}
of ES. This mechanism unblocks redexes that
otherwise would be stuck,
e.g.

(\lambda x.x)[y\backslash zz]v\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}x[x\backslash v][y\backslash zz]
fires a
\beta
-redex
by taking
\mathtt{L}=\diamond[y\backslash zz]
as the list context in
between the function
\lambda x.x
and the argument
v
. Another
example is
(xx)[x\backslash\mathtt{I}[y\backslash zz]]\mapsto_{{\tt sV}}(\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})[y\backslash zz]
which substitutes the value
\mathtt{I}
for the
variable
x
by pushing out the list context
\mathtt{L}=\diamond[y\backslash zz]
.

The
\mathrm{v}_{k}
-stratified reduction

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
is the

\mathtt{V}_{k}
-closure of the two rewriting rules

\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}
and
\mapsto_{{\tt sV}}
. In particular,

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}
) is called

surface
(resp.
full
)
reduction
. We
write
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
for the reflexive and transitive
closure

of the reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
, and
\rightarrow^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
(with
i\in\mathbb{N}
) for the

composition

i
times of
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
. As an example, consider

\begin{array}[]{l}\lambda x.\big(\lambda y.xw\big)[w\backslash\lambda z.\Omega]\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{2}}\;\lambda x.\lambda y.\big(x\,\lambda z.\Omega\big)\\
\hskip 14.22636pt\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{3}}\;\lambda x.\lambda y.\big(x\,\lambda z.((ww)[w\backslash\Delta])\big)\;\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{3}}\;\lambda x.\lambda y.\big(x\,\lambda z.\Omega\big)\end{array}

The first and third reduction steps are

{\tt sV}
-steps while the second is a

{\tt dB}
-step. Moreover, the first step is indeed a

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
-step for any
k\geq 2
. However, the
second and third steps are not
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
-steps for
0\leq k\leq 2
.
In general,
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{i}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. For
k\notin\{0,\omega\}
,
\mathrm{v}_{k}
-stratified reduction is not confluent.

Lemma 2.2 (
(
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
)
).

The
dCBV
surface and full reductions
are
confluent
.

Normal Forms.

A term
t
is
\mathrm{v}_{k}
-
normal
(or a

\mathrm{v}_{k}
-normal form
), noted

{\tt NF}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}(t)
, if there is no
u
such that
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}u
. Note that
{\tt NF}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}(t)

implies
{\tt NF}_{\mathrm{v}_{i}}(t)
. A term
t
is a

surface
(resp.
full
)
normal form
if
it is
\mathrm{v}_{0}
-normal (resp.

\mathrm{v}_{\omega}
-normal). A term
t
is

\mathrm{v}_{k}
-normalizing
if
t\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}u
for some
\mathrm{v}_{k}
-normal
form
u
. In particular, a term
t
is
surface
(resp.
full
)

normalizing
if it is

\mathrm{v}_{0}
-normalizing (resp.

\mathrm{v}_{\omega}
-normalizing). Since
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{i}}\subsetneq\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}
, then some terms may be

\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{i}}
-normalizing but not

\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}
-normalizing,
e.g.

\lambda x.((\lambda z.xy)[y\backslash\lambda w.\Omega])
is

\mathrm{v}_{0}
,
\mathrm{v}_{1}
and

\mathrm{v}_{2}
-normalizing but not

\mathrm{v}_{3}
-normalizing. As a consequence, there are terms
that are surface but not full normalizing.

Normal forms can be
syntactically
characterized by the
following grammar
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
(
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
),
where the subgrammar
{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\ (k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega})

generates special normal forms called
neutral
, which never
create a redex when applied to an argument.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}&\coloneqq&x\;|\;{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}[x\backslash{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}]\\[5.69046pt]
{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}&\coloneqq&{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\,{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\,{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}[x\backslash{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}]\\[5.69046pt]
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}&\coloneqq&\lambda x.t\;|\;{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}\;|\;{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}[x\backslash{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}]\\
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}&\coloneqq&\lambda x.{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{i}}\;|\;{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}\;|\;{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}[x\backslash{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{i+1}}]\;(i\in\mathbb{N})\\
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}&\coloneqq&\lambda x.{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt vr}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}[x\backslash{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}]\end{array}

Lemma 2.3.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
. Then
t\in\Lambda
is
\mathrm{v}_{k}
-normal iff
t\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
.

Quantitative Types.

We now present the
quantitative typing system
\mathcal{V}
, originally introduced
in
(
Ehrhard 2012
)
for the
\lambda
-calculus
and extended to
dCBV
in
(
Bucciarelli et al. 2020
)
. It
contains functional and intersection types. Intersection is
considered to be associative, commutative but not idempotent, thus an
intersection type is represented by a (possibly empty) finite multiset

\left[\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}
. Formally, given a countably infinite set

\mathcal{TV}
of type variables
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\dots
,
we inductively define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma,\tau,\rho&\Coloneqq&\alpha\in\mathcal{TV}\;|\;\mathcal{M}\;|\;\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\\
\mathcal{M}&\Coloneqq&\left[\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\ (I\mbox{ finite})\end{array}

The typing rule of system
\mathcal{V}
are defined as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.04854pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt var})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$x:\mathcal{M}\vdash x:\mathcal{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 14.22636pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.26448pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt abs})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=6.94444pt,depth=1.94444pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{$\Pi_{i}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\mathrel{}$}$\mathrel{}\Gamma_{i};x:\mathcal{M}_{i}\vdash t:\sigma_{i}$}}}$}\right)_{i\in I}$}}}\hbox{\hbox{$\;\;+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{}$}$\mathrel{}\vdash\lambda x.t:\left[\mathcal{M}_{i}\Rightarrow\sigma_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\;\;$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt app})$}}}\hbox{\hskip 8.74721pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma_{1}\vdash t:\left[\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\right]\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Pi_{2}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma_{2}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash tu:\sigma$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 1.6104pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt es})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma_{1};x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Pi_{2}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma_{2}\vdash u:\mathcal{M}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}\vdash t[x\backslash u]:\sigma$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

with
I
finite in rule
({\tt abs})
. A
(type)
derivation
is a tree obtained by applying the (inductive) typing
rules of system
\mathcal{V}
. The notation
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
means there is a derivation of the judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
in system
\mathcal{V}
. We say that
t
is

\mathcal{V}
-typable
if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
holds for some
\Gamma,\sigma
. For example, the term

\lambda x.yz
is typable
with the context
\Gamma=y:\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right],z:\left[\,\right]
and type

\alpha
, as illustrated below.

(1)

({\tt abs})

({\tt app})

({\tt abs})

({\tt var})

y:\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]\vdash y:\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]

\vdash\lambda z.\Omega:\left[\,\right]

y:\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]\vdash y(\lambda z.\Omega):\alpha

y:\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]\vdash\lambda x.y(\lambda z.\Omega):\left[\left[\,\right]\Rightarrow\alpha\right]

System
\mathcal{V}

characterizes
surface normalization
(see
Lemma

2.5
).

Meaningfulness.

Meaningfulness is about the
possibility to differentiate operationally relevant terms from those
that are irrelevant. Operational relevance can be understood as the
ability, under appropriate circumstances, to produce some element that
is
observable
within the corresponding language. Scrutability
in
dCBV

(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
aligns precisely with this
principle. Throughout this paper, call-by-value scrutability is called

meaningfulness
. Formally,

Definition 2.4.

A term
t\in\Lambda
is said
dCBV
-
meaningful
if
there exists a testing context
\mathtt{T}
and a value
v\in\Upsilon
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}v
, where
testing contexts are defined by
\mathtt{T}\;\Coloneqq\;\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{T}\,u\;|\;(\lambda x.\mathtt{T})\,u
.

The intuition behind meaningfulness is that values are the observable
elements in call-by-value, while testing contexts
\mathtt{T}
supply
arguments to their plugged term, possibly binding them to variables.
For example
t=x(\lambda y.z)
is meaningful as

\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}\lambda y.z
for
\mathtt{T}=(\lambda x.\diamond)(\lambda z.z)
, while
\Omega
and

x\Omega
are meaningless as for whatever testing context
\Omega
and
x\Omega
are plugged into,
\Omega
will not be erased.

Meaningfulness can be characterized
operationally
,
through the notion of surface-normalization, and
logically
,
through typability in type system
\mathcal{V}
. The logical
characterization of meaningfulness plays a central role in this work
as it yields the initial genericity result
(
Lemma

2.7
) upon which
all the other results are founded.

Lemma 2.5 (
dCBV
Meaningful Characterizations
(
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
;
Bucciarelli et al. 2020
)
).

Let
t\in\Lambda
. Then the following characterizations hold:

(Operational)

t
is
dCBV
-meaningful iff
t
is
dCBV
surface-normalizing.

(Logical)

t
is
dCBV
-meaningful iff
t
is

\mathcal{V}
-typable.

Adequacy of the
dCBV
-Calculus.

Plotkin’s seminal work
(
Plotkin 1975
)
established the foundations
and key concepts about the
call-by-value

\lambda
-calculus
(
CbV
). The syntax of Plotkin’s CbV is based on
ES-free terms. Using the notations introduced above, the reduction relations
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{pv}_{0}}

and
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}
in Plotkin’s CbV are the closures
under ES-free surface and full contexts, of the
{\tt\beta_{v}}
-rule
below:

(\lambda x.t)\,v\;\mapsto_{{\tt\beta_{v}}}\;t\{x\backslash v\}\quad\text{where $v$ is a ES-free\ value}

For example,
(\lambda x.x)\lambda y.z\rightarrow_{\mathrm{pv}_{0}}\lambda y.z
and
\lambda x.((\lambda y.z)x)\rightarrow_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}\lambda x.z
. As expected,

\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}
denotes the reflexive-transitive
closure of
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}
.
It is straightforward to see that reduction in Plotkin’s CbV can be
easily simulated by reductions in
dCBV
.
The converse fails (hence
dCBV
reduces
more
that Plotkin’s CbV), even when considering ES-free terms: take

t\coloneqq(\lambda x.\Delta)(yy)\Delta
,
where
\delta\coloneqq\lambda z.zz
. Note that

\lambda x.\Delta
is applied to
yy
—which is not a
value and cannot reduce to a value— so
t
is normal in
Plotkin’s CbV, but it diverges in
dCBV
:

\displaystyle t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}{\Delta}[x\backslash yy]\Delta\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}(zz)[z\backslash\Delta][x\backslash yy]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}\Delta\Delta[x\backslash yy]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}\dots

Concerning the notion of meaningfulness, it can be adapted to Plotkin’s CbV: a ES-free term is
Plotkin-meaningful
if there is a testing context
\mathtt{T}
and a ES-free value
v
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{pv}_{0}}v
.

Plotkin
(
Plotkin 1975
)
also introduced the notion of

observational equivalence
to identify ES-free terms having the
same behavior. Two ES-free terms
t,u
are
observationally
equivalent in Plotkin’s CbV
, noted
t\cong^{p}u
, if for every ES-free full context
\mathtt{C}
such that
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>

are
closed
,
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}v_{1}

iff
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{pv}_{\omega}}v_{2}
for some ES-free values
v_{1},v_{2}
. This notion can be naturally extended to ES: two
terms
t,u\in\Lambda
are
observationally equivalent in

dCBV
, noted
t\cong u
, if for every full context
\mathtt{C}

such that
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
are
closed
,

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}v_{1}
iff
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}v_{2}
for some values
v_{1},v_{2}
. Accattoli
and Guerrieri
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
proved that the notions
of meaningfulness and observational equivalence coincide in
Plotkin’s CbV and
dCBV
.

Lemma 2.6 (
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
).

Let
t,u
be two ES-free terms.

•

Observational equivalence in Plotkin and
dCBV
coincide.

•

t
is Plotkin-meaningful if and only if
t
is

dCBV
-meaningful.

Thus, the semantic notions of meaningfulness and observational
equivalence are
robust
in CbV, they do not depend on the
syntactic formulation of the calculus (Plotkin’s or
dCBV
). Said
differently,
dCBV
is
conservative
with respect to Plotkin’s
CbV: it does not change its semantics. However, there is a crucial
difference between Plotkin’s CbV and
dCBV
. As Paolini and Ronchi
Della Rocca
(
Paolini and Ronchi Della Rocca 1999
)
first observed (see
also
(
Rocca and Paolini 2004
;
Accattoli and Paolini 2012
;
Carraro and Guerrieri 2014
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2016
;
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)
),
Plotkin’s CbV is not
adequate
:

(1)

observational equivalence may equate terms having different
operational behaviors in Plotkin’s CbV, normal form and a diverging term,
e.g.

t\coloneqq(\lambda x.\Delta)(yy)\Delta
seen above is
normal, but
t\cong\Delta\Delta
and
\Delta\Delta
is
diverging;

(2)

meaningfulness cannot be characterized operationally inside
Plotkin’s CbV, for instance again the term
t\coloneqq(\lambda x.\Delta)(yy)\Delta
seen above is
normal for Plotkin, but meaningless.

The inadequacy of Plotkin’s CbV is due to
open
terms, that is,
terms containing free variables (such as
t
above). Indeed, Plotkin’s
CbV works well only when reduction is
weak
(it does not go
under abstractions) and restricted to
closed
terms (without
free variables): this is enough to properly model evaluation of
functional programs. But Plotkin’s does not model adequately
proof-assistants
(
Grégoire and Leroy 2002
)
, where evaluation proceeds
under abstraction, and hence on possibly (locally) open terms. On the
contrary,
dCBV
is adequate because meaningfulness can be
characterized operationally inside the calculus
(
Lemma

2.5
) and observational
equivalence does not equate terms with a different operational
behavior (
Section

6
).

Surface Genericity.

Genericity can be stated in
various ways, according to the previous different characterizations of
meaningfulness (
Lemma

2.5
). As
we shall see, an operational presentation of genericity is more
informative than a logical one, it particularly allows for the
extraction of quantitative information of the reduction sequences to
normal form, as well as observational information about these normal
forms. Nonetheless, proving logical genericity, called here

typed genericity
, is a more straightforward endeavor (just an
induction on full contexts), so that we start with it.

Lemma 2.7 (
dCBV
Typed Genericity).

Let
t\in\Lambda
be
dCBV
-meaningless. If
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>:\sigma
, then there
is
\Pi^{\prime}\triangleright_{\mathcal{V}}\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>:\sigma
for all
u\in\Lambda
.

For instance, in the derivation (
1
), we can replace
\Omega
, which is
dCBV
-meaningless, with any
u\in\Lambda
without changing any type.

The logical characterization of meaningfulness
(
Lemma

2.5
) together with the
previous
dCBV
Typed Genericity result suffice to derive the
following first (weak) surface genericity theorem.

Theorem 2.8 (
dCBV
Qualitative Surface Genericity).

Let
t\in\Lambda
be
dCBV
-meaningless. If
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
is

dCBV
-meaningful then
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
is also

dCBV
-meaningful for all
u\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

Let
t
be
dCBV
-meaningless and

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
be

dCBV
-meaningful. By

Lemma

2.5
,

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
is

\mathcal{V}
-typable. By

Lemma

2.7
,

\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
is also

\mathcal{V}
-typable, hence

\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
is

dCBV
-meaningful by

Lemma

2.5
.
∎

These preliminary outcomes will be further bolstered and refined in
the subsequent sections of the paper: on one hand we will prove full
genericity, as explained in
Section

1
, on the other hand, we
will enrich the genericity property quantitatively.

3.
Towards Full Genericity: Key Tools

In this paper, we prove genericity results for
dCBV
and
dCBN
,
starting from
qualitative surface genericity
up to

quantitative stratified genericity
, thus including full
genericity as a particular case, an important result for the theory of
call-by-value that has never been proved before. All these results
stand upon a first
typed genericity
result
(
Lemma

2.7
) that we
swiftly proved using the existing logical characterization of
meaningfulness in
(
Accattoli and Guerrieri 2022
)

(
Lemma

2.5
). For the rest of our
results, we use a technique based on some additional key ingredients
that we discuss in what follows. We use a
generic
vocabulary
“calculus”, “stratified context”, “stratified reduction”, etc, the
intention being that the same methodology will be applied to
call-by-value (
Section

4
) and to
call-by-name (
Section

5
).

Specifying Unknown Subterms: Meaningful Approximants
.

The set of terms
\Lambda
of the calculus is extended with a new
constant
\bot
representing an
unknown piece of term
.
The resulting set of
partial terms
is noted
{\bf\Lambda}
,
using bold font. All the previous notions are lifted consequently:
partial stratified contexts, partial stratified reduction,
partial normalization, etc. They are all defined as conservative
extensions to partial elements of their underlying original ones.

Partial terms are endowed with a
partial order

\leq
, defined as the partial full context closure of the
relation
\bot\leq\mathbf{t}
, for all
\mathbf{t}\in{\bf\Lambda}
. Intuitively,
\mathbf{t}\leq\mathbf{u}
means that

\mathbf{u}
carries more information than
\mathbf{t}
.

We use the partial calculus to abstract from meaningless subterms. For
that purpose, for each term
t
we construct its
meaningful approximant

\mathcal{MA}(t)
, a partial term obtained by replacing all meaningless subterms of
t
with
\bot
.
As expected,
\mathcal{MA}(t)
(under-)approximates
t
, in
that
\mathcal{MA}(t)\leq t
. Meaningful approximants are the
cornerstone of our approach: they will be used as intermediary
stakeholders to transfer a reduction sequence between two terms into
another reduction sequence between two other related terms.

Axioms
.

The method used to derive quantitative stratified genericity from the
preliminary typed genericity result incorporates all the previously
mentioned elements, in addition to some general statements on the
partial calculus and meaningful approximants. These statements,
called
axioms
,
uniformly
outline the prerequisites
that are necessary for achieving quantitative and full genericity from
qualitative and surface genericity. Nonetheless, they require
validation for each individual calculus, given that their suitability
is closely tied to their respective concept of stratified reduction.

We
now detail the four axioms.
Our approach strongly relies on the operational characterization of
meaningfulness. Thus, all the axioms are related to
reduction sequences and normal forms.

Numerous natural approaches exist to extend a given calculus into some
corresponding partial calculus
(
Sections

4.1

and

5.2
).
However, not all of these methods consistently allow for an accurate
approximation of the calculus’s dynamic behavior. What we mean by this
is the capacity to relate reductions in the calculus to those
occurring in its partial counterpart, and vice versa.

We assume that
k
-reduction sequences on terms can be
approximated by means of
k
-reduction sequences on meaningful
approximants. Ideally, this means that
t\rightarrow_{k}u
implies

\mathcal{MA}(t)\rightarrow_{k}\mathcal{MA}(u)
. However, this
is not always true in our frameworks, and it is sufficient to
approximate reduction sequences in the following weaker way.
We denote by
\rightarrow^{=}_{k}
the reflexive closure of

\rightarrow_{k}
.

Axiom

A
(Dynamic Approximation)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
t,u\in\Lambda
.
If
t\rightarrow_{k}u
then there is

\mathbf{u}\in{\bf\Lambda}
such that
\mathcal{MA}(t)\rightarrow_{k}^{=}\mathbf{u}\geq\mathcal{MA}(u)
.

We also assume that the partial order gives a natural lifting of
partial
k
-reduction sequences into other reduction sequence
(on terms or partial terms) of same length.

Axiom

B
(Dynamic Partial Lifting)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in{\bf\Lambda}
. If
\mathbf{t}\rightarrow_{k}\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{t}\leq\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\rightarrow_{k}\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{u}
for some
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\in{\bf\Lambda}
.

Stratified normal forms are exactly the meaningful and observable
outcomes behind the notion of stratified computation. Let

{\tt no}_{k}
be the set of
k
-normal forms.
Partial
k
-normal forms without any occurrence of
\bot
at
depth
k
, noted
{\tt\textbf{bno}}_{k}
, provide perfect
approximations of
k
-normal forms by keeping all their
meaningful content. Our next axiom asserts that the meaningful
approximant of an
k
-normal form does not loose any meaningful
information.

Axiom

C
(Observability of Normal Form Approximants)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{t}\in{\tt no}_{k}
, then
\mathcal{MA}(t)\in{\tt\textbf{bno}}_{k}
.

Finally, partial terms in
{\tt\textbf{bno}}_{k}
are supposed
to provide perfect approximants for
k
-normal forms. The last
axiom asserts that they are stable by increasing information.

Axiom

D
(Stability of Meaningful Observables)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{t}\in{\tt\textbf{bno}}_{k}
. If
\mathbf{u}\in{\bf\Lambda}

and
\mathbf{t}\leq\mathbf{u}
, then
\mathbf{u}\in{\tt\textbf{bno}}_{k}
and
\mathbf{t}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}\mathbf{u}
.

4.
Call-by-Value Stratified Genericity

In this section we first introduce the
partial

dCBV
-calculus (
Section

4.1
), an extension
of
dCBV
enabling the simplification of some (meaningless)
subterms. Then, we prove that the axioms presented in

Section

3
hold in the partial
dCBV
(
Lemmas

4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.10
).
We use these properties to strengthen our first
dCBV
qualitative
surface genericity result into a quantitative stratified one
(
Theorem

4.11
).

4.1.
The Partial
dCBV
-Calculus

Syntax.

Terms of the partial
dCBV
-calculus share the same structure as those of
dCBV
, with
the additional constant
\bot
. Formally, the sets

\mathbf{\Lambda}\supsetneq\Lambda
of
partial terms
and

\mathbf{\Upsilon}\supsetneq\Upsilon
of
partial values

for the partial
dCBV
-calculus, denoted by the same symbols for
terms but distinguished by a bold font, are defined inductively as
follows:

\begin{array}[]{r rcl}\textbf{(Partial terms)}&\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u}&\Coloneqq&\mathbf{v}\;|\;\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{u}\;|\;\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{u}]\;|\;\bot\\
\textbf{(Partial values)}&\mathbf{v}&\Coloneqq&x\in\mathcal{X}\;|\;\lambda x.\mathbf{t}\end{array}

Contexts.

As in
dCBV
, there is a stratified
operational semantics, but now defined by means of partial contexts.
The sets of
partial list contexts

(\mathbf{L})
and

partial stratified contexts

(\mathbf{V}_{k})
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, are inductively defined as follows (where
i\in\mathbb{N}
):

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{L}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathbf{L}[x\backslash\mathbf{t}]\\[5.69046pt]
\mathbf{V}_{0}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{0}\,\mathbf{t}\;|\;\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{V}_{0}\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{0}[x\backslash\mathbf{t}]\;|\;\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{V}_{0}]\\
\mathbf{V}_{i+1}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathbf{V}_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{i+1}\,\mathbf{t}\;|\;\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{V}_{i+1}\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{i+1}[x\backslash\mathbf{t}]\;|\;\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{V}_{i+1}]\\
\mathbf{V}_{\omega}&\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathbf{V}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{\omega}\,\mathbf{t}\;|\;\mathbf{t}\,\mathbf{V}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathbf{V}_{\omega}[x\backslash\mathbf{t}]\;|\;\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{V}_{\omega}]\end{array}\end{array}

In particular, we call
partial surface
(resp.
full
)

context
the base (resp. transfinite) level
\mathbf{V}_{0}

(resp.
\mathbf{V}_{\omega}
).

(Stratified) Operational Semantics.

The
following rewriting rules are the base components of our reduction
relations. Any partial term having the shape of the left-hand side of
one of these three rules is called a
redex
.

\begin{array}[]{c c c}\mathbf{L}\!\left<\lambda x.\mathbf{t}\right>\mathbf{u}\;\mapsto_{{\tt\textbf{dB}}}\;\mathbf{L}\!\left<\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{u}]\right>&&\mathbf{t}[x\backslash\mathbf{L}\!\left<\mathbf{v}\right>]\;\mapsto_{{\tt\textbf{sV}}}\;\mathbf{L}\!\left<\mathbf{t}\{x\backslash\mathbf{v}\}\right>\end{array}

Building upon the established pattern used for terms, the

stratified partial reduction
(or
\textbf{v}_{k}
-reduction in bold)
\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
is the partial
k
-stratified closure (
\mathbf{V}_{k}
-closure) of the
two rewriting rules
\mapsto_{{\tt\textbf{dB}}}
and

\mapsto_{{\tt\textbf{sV}}}
. In particular, the reduction

\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{0}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}
) is called

partial surface
(resp.
full
)
reduction
. Its
reflexive and transitive closure is again denoted by
\rightarrow^{*}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
. A partial term
\mathbf{t}
is a
\textbf{v}_{k}
-normal form
(or
\textbf{v}_{k}
-normal
) if
there is no
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
such that
\mathbf{t}\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\mathbf{u}
. A partial term is

\textbf{v}_{k}
-normalizing
if
\mathbf{t}\rightarrow^{*}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\mathbf{u}
for some

\textbf{v}_{k}
-normal form
\mathbf{u}
, and similarly for the
partial surface and full reductions.

Example 4.1.

\mathbf{t}_{0}=\big(\lambda y.\bot w\big)[w\backslash\lambda z.\bot]\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\lambda y.\bot\,\big(\lambda z.\bot\big)=\mathbf{t}_{1}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
. In particular,

\mathbf{t}_{1}
is partial surface normal.

4.2.
Quantitative Stratified Genericity

Meaningful Approximation.

As explained before,
our approach is based on abstracting meaningless subterms by means of
partial terms. This is implemented through the tool of

meaningful approximation
, which associates with each term a
partial term obtained by replacing all its meaningless subterms with

\bot
. Formally, the

dCBV
-meaningful approximant

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)
of a term
t\in\Lambda
is defined as

\bot
if
t
is
dCBV
-meaningless, and inductively
on
t
as follows otherwise:

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\hskip-8.5359pt\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(x\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq x

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092pt\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t[x\backslash u]\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)[x\backslash\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(u\right)]

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\lambda x.t\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\lambda x.\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092pt\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(tu\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(u\right)

Example 4.2.

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left((\lambda x.x\Omega)(\lambda y.\Omega)\right)=(\lambda x.\bot)(\lambda y.\bot)
.

Another partial calculus already exists in a CbV
setting
(
Kerinec et al. 2020
;
Kerinec et al. 2021
;
ManzonettoPR2019
)
,
which only approximates values. Their approach seems incompatible with
our definition of meaningful approximant: it cannot be used to
approximate meaningless terms such as
\Omega
.

We equip the set of partial terms
{\bf\Lambda}
with a

partial order

\leq
defined as the partial full
context closure of
\bot\leq\mathbf{t}
for any partial term
\mathbf{t}\in{\bf\Lambda}
. This definition does not only allow us
to approximate values, as done in
(
Kerinec et al. 2020
)
,
but any kind of term. As expected, the meaningful approximation of a
term approximates it.

Lemma 4.3.

Let
t\in\Lambda
, then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)\leq t
.

Using qualitative surface genericity
(
Theorem

2.8
), we obtain a
genericity result for meaningful approximants which states that
replacing a meaningless subterm by any other kind of term can only
refine its meaningful approximant.

Lemma 4.4 (
dCBV
Approximant Genericity).

Let
\mathtt{C}
be a full context. If
t\in\Lambda

is
dCBV
-meaningless, then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)\leq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>\right)
for all
u\in\Lambda
.

Example 4.5.

One has
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left((\lambda x.x\Omega)(\lambda y.\Omega)\right)=(\lambda x.\bot)(\lambda y.\bot)\leq(\lambda x.x\mathtt{I})(\lambda y.\bot)=\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left((\lambda x.x\mathtt{I})(\lambda y.\Omega)\right)
since
\mathtt{I}
is
dCBV
-meaningful.

The previous property constitutes the core of our quantitative
stratified genericity result. It will be used in particular to
construct a common partial term approximating two distinct terms

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
, where
t
is necessarily a
meaningless subterm. This common partial term will then be used to
transform a reduction sequence from
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
into a reduction
sequence from
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
.

Dynamic Approximation and Lifting.

We now seek
to transfer reduction sequences between two terms by using a common
partial term approximating both of them.
First, we show that term reduction steps are simulated by the
corresponding meaningful approximants. This corresponds to

A
for
dCBV
.

Lemma 4.6 (
dCBV
Dynamic Approximation).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
t,u\!\in\!\Lambda
.
If
t\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}\!u
then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)\rightarrow^{=}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\!\mathbf{u}\geq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(u\right)
for some
\mathbf{u}\!\in\!\mathbf{\Lambda}
.

Thus, the meaningful approximant
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)
replicates the

essence
of the reduction sequence from
t
. Still, there are two
important differences. First, some steps may be
equated by their meaningful approximants,
e.g.

t_{0}\coloneqq\Omega\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}(xx)[x\backslash\Delta]\eqqcolon t_{1}
while

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)=\bot=\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{1}\right)
. Secondly, some
reduction steps do not yield the meaningful approximation but an
over-approximation of it,
e.g.
consider
t_{2}\coloneqq(x(\lambda y.z\Delta))[z\backslash\Delta]\rightarrow_{\mathrm{v}_{0}}x(\lambda y.\Omega)\eqqcolon t_{3}
, then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{1}\right)=(x(\lambda y.z\Delta))[z\backslash\Delta]\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{0}}x(\lambda y.\Omega)
while
x(\lambda y.\Omega)\geq x\bot=\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{3}\right)
.

Partial reduction sequences can also be lifted to greater partial terms, and in particular to terms. This is

B
for

dCBV
.

Lemma 4.7 (
dCBV
Dynamic Partial Lift).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{t},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbf{\Lambda}
. If
\mathbf{t}\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\!\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{t}\leq\mathbf{t}^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\!\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\!\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\!\geq\mathbf{u}

for some
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}\!\in\!\mathbf{\Lambda}
.

Recalling
Example

4.1
, let

\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{0}\coloneqq\big(\lambda y.\mathtt{I}w\big)[w\backslash\lambda z.\lambda x.\bot]
,
so that
\mathbf{t}_{0}\leq\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{0}
. Then
\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\lambda y.\mathtt{I}\,\big(\lambda z.\lambda x.\bot\big)=:\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, and
\mathbf{t}_{1}\leq\mathbf{t}^{\prime}_{1}
.

Observability.

Stratified normal forms are
precisely the meaningful and observable outcomes behind the notion of
stratified computation. For
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, the set
{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
of partial terms introduced below aims
to provide perfect approximation of them, being

\textbf{v}_{k}
-normal forms without any occurrence of

\bot
at depth
k
.

\displaystyle{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,x\;|\;{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}[x\backslash{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}]

\displaystyle{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\,{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\;|\;{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\,{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\;|\;{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}[x\backslash{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}]

\displaystyle{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{0}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,\lambda x.\mathbf{t}\;|\;{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{0}}\;|\;{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{0}}\;|\;{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{0}}[x\backslash{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{0}}]

\displaystyle{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,\lambda x.{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{i}}\;|\;{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{i+1}}\;|\;{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{i+1}}\;|\;{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{i+1}}[x\backslash{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{i+1}}]\ \ (i\in\mathbb{N})

\displaystyle{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\,\lambda x.{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt bvr}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}\;|\;{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}[x\backslash{\tt bne}_{\textbf{v}_{\omega}}]

Remarkably, our meaningful approximants are precise enough to
perfectly observe the important level of these meaningful results. We
thus obtain the
dCBV
instance of

C
.

Lemma 4.8 (
dCBV
Observability of Normal Form Approximants).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
and
\mathbf{t}\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
, then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t\right)\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
.

Example 4.9.

t_{0}\coloneqq\lambda x.(x(\lambda y.\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})(\lambda z.\mathtt{I}\Omega))

is
\mathrm{v}_{1}
-normal and its meaningful approximant is

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)=\lambda x.(x(\lambda y.\mathtt{I}\mathtt{I})(\lambda z.\bot))\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{1}}
.

Finally, we show that partial terms in
{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}

entirely determinate the first
k
levels of the

k
-normal forms they approximate. Consequently, any greater
approximant aligns with its first
k
levels. This is the

dCBV
precisely the instance of

D
.

Lemma 4.10 (
dCBV
Stability of Meaningful Observables).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
. If
\mathbf{t}\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
and
\mathbf{t}\leq\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
, then
\mathbf{u}\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
and

\mathbf{t}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}\mathbf{u}
.

Note that
\mathbf{u}
can be a term. Taking

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)
from
Example

4.9
, where

\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)\leq t_{0}
as expected, one verifies that both
partial terms
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)
and
t_{0}
share the same structure
on the first
2
-levels. And similarly for any other partial term
greater than
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(t_{0}\right)
.

Quantitative Stratified Genericity.

We now have
all the material to enhance our first
qualitative
surface
genericity (
Theorem

2.8
) into a
stronger
quantitative
stratified genericity result.

Theorem 4.11 (
dCBV
Quantitative Stratified Genericity).

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
. If
t\in\Lambda
is

dCBV
-meaningless and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
is

\mathrm{v}_{k}
-normalizing, then there is
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
u\in\Lambda
, there are

t^{\prime},u^{\prime}\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
such that
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}t^{\prime}\;\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}\;u^{\prime}\;\sideset{{}^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}\!\!\!\!}}{}{\revArr}\;\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
.

Proof.

By definition, there is
s\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
such that

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}s
. By iterating

Lemmas

4.6

and

4.7
,
there are
\mathbf{s}\in\mathbf{\Lambda}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}

such that
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)\rightarrow^{i}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}\mathbf{s}\geq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(s\right)
. Since
s\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
,
then
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(s\right)\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
using

Lemma

4.8
, thus
\mathbf{s}\in{\tt bno}_{\textbf{v}_{k}}
using

Lemma

4.10
.
On one hand,
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)\leq\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>

by
Lemma

4.3
, thus by
iterating
Lemma

4.7
, one has

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}t^{\prime}
for some
t^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that
\mathbf{s}\leq t^{\prime}
. By

Lemma

4.10
,
t^{\prime}\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
and
t^{\prime}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}\mathbf{s}
. On
the other hand,
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)\leq\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>\right)
according to

Lemma

4.4
,
thus
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)\leq\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
by

Lemma

4.3
and transitivity. By iterating

Lemma

4.7
again, one has that

\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>\rightarrow^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}u^{\prime}
for some
u^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that
\mathbf{s}\leq u^{\prime}
. By

Lemma

4.10
,
u^{\prime}\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
and
u^{\prime}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}\mathbf{s}
.
Finally,
t^{\prime}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}u^{\prime}
by transitivity of
\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}
.
∎

Graphically, these are the main steps in the proof of
Theorem

4.11
:
\mathcal{M\!A}_{{\tt V}}\!\left(\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\right)
\mathbf{s}\!
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>
t^{\prime}\!
\ni
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
u^{\prime}\!
\in
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{v}_{k}}
\scriptscriptstyle n
\scriptscriptstyle\textbf{v}_{k}
\scriptscriptstyle n
\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{v}_{k}
\scriptscriptstyle n
\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{v}_{k}
\leftsquigarrow
\scriptscriptstyle{\tt Lem}.\ref{lem:cbvAGK_Aprx_Simulation_MultiStep}
\rightsquigarrow
\scriptscriptstyle{\tt Lem}.\ref{lem:cbvAGK_Aprx_Simulation_MultiStep}
\geq
\scriptscriptstyle{\tt Lem.}\ref{lem:Cbv_MA(t)_<=_t}
\geq
\leq
\scriptscriptstyle{\tt Lem.}\ref{lem:Cbv_t_meaningless_=>_MA(F<t>)_<=_MA(F<u>)}\;\&\;{\tt Lem.}\ref{lem:Cbv_MA(t)_<=_t}
\leq
\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\scalebox{4}[1]{$\approx$}}
\scriptscriptstyle{\tt Lem.}\ref{lem:Cbv_|t_in_bnoSn_and_t_<=_u_==>_u_in_BnoSn}

A particular case is given by the following statement since

k
-equality for
k=\omega
is just the equality
between terms.

Corollary 4.12 (
dCBV
Quantitative Full Genericity).

If
t\in\Lambda
is
dCBV
-meaningless and
\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}s
where
s
is

\mathrm{v}_{\omega}
-normal, then there is
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
u\in\Lambda
,

\mathtt{C}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}s\;\sideset{{}^{i}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}\!\!\!\!}}{}{\revArr}\;\mathtt{C}\!\left<u\right>
.

For instance,
(\lambda y.\mathtt{I})\lambda z.\Omega\rightarrow^{2}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}}\mathtt{I}\;\sideset{{}^{2}_{\mathrm{v}_{\omega}\!\!\!\!}}{}{\revArr}\;(\lambda y.\mathtt{I})\lambda z.u
for any
u\in\Lambda
.

Theorem

4.11
,

Corollary

4.12
and the general
methodology used to prove such results are among the main
contributions of this paper.

5.
Call-by-Name Stratified Genericity

Section

5.1
introduces the syntax and operational semantics
of the
dCBN
-calculus
(
Accattoli and Kesner 2010
;
Accattoli and Kesner 2012
)
, including the new notion of
stratified
reduction
. We also recall the
\mathcal{N}
type system
(
Kesner and Ventura 2014
)
. The
qualitative and quantitative stratified genericity results follow in

Section

5.2
.

5.1.
Syntax and Stratified Operational Semantics

Syntax.

The term syntax of the
dCBN
-calculus
is exactly the same as in
dCBV
. We keep the same notations for
terms (
s,t,u,\dots
) and the set of terms (
\Lambda
). In the
operational semantics of
dCBN
(see below), values do not play any
role, so we do not distinguish them. Free variables,

\alpha
-conversion and substitution are defined as in
dCBV
.

Contexts.

The operational semantics of the

dCBN
-calculus is often specified by means of a
head

reduction that forbids evaluation inside arguments, called here

surface
reduction. Here again, we equip the
dCBN
-calculus
with a new
stratified
operational semantics which generalizes
surface reduction to different
levels
.

The set of
list contexts

(\mathtt{L})
and
stratified
contexts

(\mathtt{N}_{k})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, that
can be seen as terms containing exactly one hole
\diamond
, are
inductively defined as follows (where
i\in\mathbb{N}
):

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathtt{L}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{L}[x\backslash t]\\[5.69046pt]
\mathtt{N}_{0}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{N}_{0}\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{0}\,t\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{0}[x\backslash t]\\
\mathtt{N}_{i+1}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{N}_{i+1}\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{i+1}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{N}_{i}\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{i+1}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{N}_{i}]\\
\mathtt{N}_{\omega},\mathtt{C}&\;\Coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\lambda x.\mathtt{N}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{\omega}\,t\;|\;t\,\mathtt{N}_{\omega}\;|\;\mathtt{N}_{\omega}[x\backslash t]\;|\;t[x\backslash\mathtt{N}_{\omega}]\end{array}

In particular,
surface contexts
(whose hole is not in an
argument) and
full contexts
(the restriction-free one-hole
contexts, also denoted by
\mathtt{C}
) are special cases of stratified context: surface contexts
are base level
\mathtt{N}_{0}
and full contexts the transfinite level

\mathtt{N}_{\omega}
. In stratified contexts
\mathtt{N}_{k}
,
the hole can occur everywhere as long as its depth is at most

k
, while in
\mathtt{N}_{\omega}
it can occur everywhere
and in
\mathtt{N}_{0}
it cannot occur in a top level argument. Thus for
example
y\,(\lambda x.\diamond)
is in
\mathtt{N}_{1}
, and thus
in every
\mathtt{N}_{k}
with
k\geq 1
, including

\mathtt{N}_{\omega}
, but it is not in

\mathtt{N}_{0}
. For all
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
, we write
\mathtt{N}_{k}\!\left<t\right>
for the term obtained
by replacing the hole in
\mathtt{N}_{k}
with the term
t
, and
similarly for
\mathtt{L}
.

We define the
stratified equality
comparing terms up to a
given depth. It is defined by the following inductive rules (with

i\in\mathbb{N})
:

​​
({\tt var})

x\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}x

​​
({\tt abs})

t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}u
\lambda x.t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}\lambda x.u

​​
({\tt app})

t_{1}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}u_{1}
t_{1}t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}u_{1}u_{2}

​​
({\tt app})

t_{1}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}{\approx}u_{1}
t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}u_{2}
t_{1}t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}{\approx}u_{1}u_{2}

​​
({\tt app})

t_{1}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u_{1}
t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u_{2}
t_{1}t_{2}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u_{1}u_{2}

​​
({\tt es})

t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}u
t[x\backslash t^{\prime}]\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}u[x\backslash u^{\prime}]

​​
({\tt es})

t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}{\approx}u
t^{\prime}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i}{\approx}u^{\prime}
t[x\backslash t^{\prime}]\overset{\scriptscriptstyle i+1}{\approx}u[x\backslash u^{\prime}]

​​
({\tt es})

t\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u
t^{\prime}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u^{\prime}
t[x\backslash t^{\prime}]\overset{\scriptscriptstyle\omega}{\approx}u[x\backslash u^{\prime}]

Example 5.1.

Let
t_{0}:=x\mathtt{I}[x\backslash y\Omega]
and
t_{1}:=x\mathtt{I}[x\backslash y\mathtt{I}]
. We have
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 0}{\approx}t_{1}

and
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle 1}{\approx}t_{1}
but not
t_{0}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle k}{\approx}t_{1}
for any

k\geq 2
.

While the same intuition behind stratification is shared between

dCBV
and
dCBN
, their respective implementations turn out to be
quite different, depending on the underlying notion of reduction.

(Stratified) Operational Semantics.

The notions
of contexts already established, we can now equip the
dCBN
with
an operational semantics acting
at a distance

(
Accattoli and Kesner 2010
;
Accattoli and Kesner 2012
)
. The
following rewriting rules are the base components of such operational
semantics.

\begin{array}[]{c c c}\mathtt{L}\!\left<\lambda x.s\right>t\;\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}\;\mathtt{L}\!\left<s[x\backslash t]\right>&&t[x\backslash u]\;\mapsto_{{\tt s}}\;t\{x\backslash u\}\end{array}

Rule
{\tt dB}
assumed to be
capture free
, so no free
variable of
t
is captured by the context
\mathtt{L}
. The main
difference between
dCBN
and
dCBV
is that rule
{\tt sV}

of the latter is replaced by rule
{\tt s}
in the former.

The
stratified reduction
(or

\mathrm{n}_{k}
-reduction
)

\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}
is the
\mathtt{N}_{k}
-closure of the two
rewriting rules
\mapsto_{{\tt dB}}
and
\mapsto_{{\tt s}}
.
In particular,
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}
(resp.
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}
) is
called
surface
(resp.
full
)
reduction
.
We write
\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}
for the reflexive and
transitive closure of reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}
.

Lemma 5.2 (
(
Accattoli and Kesner 2012
)
).

The
dCBN
surface and full reductions are

confluent
.

As in
dCBV
, For
k\notin\{0,\omega\}
,
\mathrm{n}_{k}
-reduction is not confluent.
In general,
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{i}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{i+1}}\,\subsetneq\,\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Normal Forms.

Similar to
{\tt dCBV}
, each level
of the stratified reduction
\rightarrow_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}
yields a
corresponding notion of
\mathrm{n}_{k}
-normal form

and
\mathrm{n}_{k}
-normalizability
. Normal forms can
be
syntactically
characterized by the following grammar

{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}
, where the subgrammar
{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}

generates special normal forms called
neutral
, which never
create a redex when applied to an argument.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}&\coloneqq&x\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}t\\
{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{i+1}}&\coloneqq&x\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{i+1}}{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{i}}\qquad(i\in\mathbb{N})\\
{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}&\coloneqq&x\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{\omega}}\\[5.69046pt]
{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}&\coloneqq&\lambda x.{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}\;|\;{\tt ne}_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}\qquad(k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega})\end{array}\end{array}

Lemma 5.3.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
. Then
t\in\Lambda
is
\mathrm{n}_{k}
-normal iff
t\in{\tt no}_{\mathrm{n}_{k}}
.

Quantitative Typing.

We now present the
quantitative typing system
\mathcal{N}
based on intersection
types which was introduced in
(
Kesner and Ventura 2014
)
. The typing rule of
system
\mathcal{N}
are defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 29.76262pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt var})$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$x:\left[\sigma\right]\vdash x:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\hskip 28.45274pt\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt abs})$}}}\hbox{\hskip 0.4236pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash t:\sigma$}}\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.t:\mathcal{M}\Rightarrow\sigma\quad$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt app})$}}}\hbox{\>\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=8.26085pt,depth=2.5pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma_{1}\vdash t:\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\Rightarrow\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Pi_{2}^{i}\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma_{2}^{i}\vdash u:\tau_{i}$}}}$}\right)_{i\in I}$}}}}}\hbox{\hbox{$\Gamma_{1}+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{2}^{i}\mathrel{}$}$\mathrel{}\vdash tu:\sigma\hskip 79.6678pt$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{\scriptsize$({\tt es})$}}}\hbox{\>\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{$\left(\vrule height=8.26085pt,depth=2.5pt,width=0.0pt\right.$}$\left.\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma_{1},x:\left[\tau_{i}\right]_{i\in I}\vdash t:\sigma\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Pi_{2}^{i}\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma_{2}^{i}\vdash u:\tau_{i}$}}}$}\right)_{i\in I}$}}}}}\hbox{\hbox{$\Gamma_{1}+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{2}^{i}\mathrel{}$}$\mathrel{}\vdash t[x\backslash u]:\sigma\hskip 71.13188pt$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}

with
I
finite in rules
({\tt app})
and

({\tt es})
. As with system
\mathcal{V}
, a

(type) derivation
is a tree obtained by applying the
(inductive) typing rules of system
\mathcal{N}
. Similarly, the
notation
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
means there is a
derivation of the judgment
\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
in system

\mathcal{N}
and we say that
t
is

\mathcal{N}
-typable
if
\Pi\triangleright_{\mathcal{N}}\Gamma\vdash t:\sigma
holds for some
\Gamma,\sigma
.

System
\mathcal{N}

characterizes
surface normalization
(see
Lemma

5.5
).

Meaningfulness.

We start by defining

dCBN
-meaningfulness.

Definition 5.4.

A term
t\in\Lambda
is
dCBN

meaningful
if there
is a testing context
\mathtt{T}
such that
\mathtt{T}\!\left<t\right>\rightarrow^{*}_{\mathrm{n}_{0}}\mathtt{I}
(with
\mathtt{T}\Coloneqq\diamond\;|\;\mathtt{T}\,u\;|\;(\lambda x.\mathtt{T})\,u
).
1
1

1

Usually,
dCBN
-meaningfulness (aka
solvability
)
is defined using contexts of the form
(\lambda x_{1}\dots x_{m}.\diamond)N_{1}\dots N_{n}
(
m,n\geq 0
)

(
Barendregt 1977
;
Barendregt 1984
;
Rocca and Paolini 2004
)
